- **With only one group, the UI doesn't change by a single pixel**, with one exception: the **Connection** section on the Settings home gains a "hub" row (the second hub is added from there; decided by the maintainer 2026-11-02). All three styles × light / dark were compared pixel by pixel; see verification below.
- With more than one group: computer headers in Agents get a group tag (`AgentTreeHeader(tag)`); the machine on session rows, request cards and computer chip names are written as "MacBook (Home)"; a row of group filters appears under the search box (All hubs + each group, with an online dot and session count), and the filter applies to both the list and "Needs your decision". The group filter only lasts for this page visit and isn't stored.
- Settings › hub (`HubsScreen`): one section per group, listing the group's state and each hub (paired, discovered through linking, which one is in use); the group menu has **Use for Persona and Inbox** (selected group), rename (on this phone only) and **Disconnect group**; when a group has two or more paired hubs, one can be disconnected on its own; **Add hub** goes through the original pairing page (skipping the welcome page and returning to the hub list when done), and a newly paired hub joins an existing group by mesh or becomes a new group.
- **Runs on** in Persona lists only machines in the selected group. The machine monitoring overview puts all groups together (each machine id suffixed), and details are fetched from the machine's group.
- When switching the selected group, the shell rebuilds for the new group (just like switching pairings before, each tab returns to its home): hub-level pages switch to the new group's, not a single key in the merged lists changes, and no group's connection reconnects.
- When a group goes away while pages are open (merged into another, disconnected): that group is first removed from the lists and closed only once no page uses its data anymore (`GroupStacks.awaitDropped`); calls still in flight are treated as cancelled because "the connection switched", not reported as failures; an open chat page switches to the same session's new id in place (after a merge the machine id loses its suffix), or leaves its tab's stack if it can't be found; this touches only the chat's own tab, never the tab on screen, since visited tabs stay composed and a hidden tab's chat sees the group go too (`AppNavigator.groupGone`, #5714).
- The chat page, terminal, files, sharing to a session and uploading attachments in a session all follow the session's group (`ChatWiringSource.bindingsFor`, `ShellChat`, `ShareQueue`). They used to use the single pairing's services directly, and with several groups they sent another group's sessions to the selected group's hub (actually hit: the chat page's draft version read another group's session through the selected group's repository, and the selected group's hub received suffixed requests).

### Not done yet / known limits

- Until hub linking (L1, #294) merges, real hubs' hello has no `mesh`, so every hub forms a group of its own; switching routes within a group has only been verified in unit tests and with the mock hub wrapper below.
- Device settings (notification preferences, device list), update check, phone data tools, the lock-screen test and new shares only apply to the selected group (queued shares are resent by their own group: as soon as that group is back online they're sent, without switching back; `ShareRetryWorker` also finds that group by scope and ends if the group is gone, #4818); device records live on the group's first hub, so these pages may not be readable while connected to another hub.
- How snapshots stored per pairing by `platform/shortcuts` (port/surfaces widgets and tiles) should behave with several hubs: later.
- Notifications are posted with merged ids, and opening and answering both route by group; the ongoing notification's connection state reflects only the selected group.

### Verification

- Moving pending messages and drafts on merge: `HubGroupRegistryTest` (a merge records `PendingMove` without deleting data, deletes only after moving, the move follows a group that merges again, it's deleted together with a disconnected group; which messages and drafts move, the scope change, interrupted sends become "result unclear", existing drafts aren't overwritten), and the instrumented test `HubGroupsDataStoreTest` (real Room: group b has one unsent message and a draft; after merging they move into group a's pending queue and drafts with `client_msg_id` unchanged; moving again doesn't duplicate; b's data is deleted only after moving, and b's credentials are kept).
- Unit tests: `GroupKeysTest` (suffix, primary group unchanged, hello's mesh), `HubGroupConnectionTest` (switching hubs within a group, each with its own token, borrowed credentials, the recheck switching back, a member's 401 excludes only it, only the last hub's 401 invalidates, a member address that answers as another hub isn't used, member candidates only go into `memberEndpoints`, TLS fingerprints can't be impersonated), `HubGroupRegistryTest` (migration, merge / split / names in learn, stable ids, no primary promotion, the three outcomes of adding, the erase list on disconnect, what happens to the selected group), `GroupRoutingTest` (ids in and out, draft keys, creating sessions, merged pins write only the changed group and don't overwrite unreadable groups, fleet sums), `AgentGroupsTest` (one group untagged, several groups tagged, filters). Instrumented test `HubGroupsDataStoreTest` (migration + two group scopes open at once).
- Screenshots: with only one group, 36 shots per style × light / dark compared pixel by pixel (`SHOTS_STYLE=… SHOTS_MOCK_PORT=18771 SHOTS_FROZEN=2026-11-02T04:00:00+00:00 SHOTS_SETTLE=3`); in each style only the two Settings home shots (light and dark) differ (the new "hub" row, with everything below shifted down), and the other 34 are pixel-identical (the measured-latency line in connection diagnostics is ignored as described above).
- Multiple groups (emulator, with the mock hub wrapper below, 2026-11-02):
  - Two groups (Home and Work, two mock hubs with identical data): the list is merged; the group filter, the group tag on computer headers and "MacBook (Work)" are all right. Tapping **Allow** on a Work request card: only the Work mock hub received `POST /api/v1/interactions/ix_tests_build/resolve` (without suffix), and the Home one received nothing. Opening a Work session: only the Work hub received `/items` and `/seen` (without suffix).
  - Setting Work as the group for Persona and Inbox: `/api/v1/assistant`, `/api/v1/inbox` and device preferences went to the Work hub, the Home hub's WebSocket didn't reconnect, and keys in the list didn't change.
  - Disconnecting Work: its data directory was deleted, the list went back to the one-group look (no group filter or group tags), and the Home hub's WebSocket didn't reconnect.
  - Merging two groups (first each its own group; with the Work hub stopped, send a message in a Work session and write a draft, then make each hub's mesh list the other): after the merge the chat page didn't crash and the draft appeared in the same session in the merged group. In one run the message was sent by the Work hub before the merge; in another (the App crashed during the merge and finished moving after restart) it moved into the merged group and was sent to the Home hub with its original `client_msg_id`. Either way it was sent exactly once.
  - Two hubs in one group (Home A and Home B list each other in mesh, only A paired): in the hub list B shows "discovered through linking"; stopping A switched the connection to B (B received health and the WebSocket, and B was "in use" in the hub list; single observation, about 2 seconds, not measured). After A recovered, the 5-minute recheck switched back; we didn't wait for it.
  - Opening and answering notifications wasn't tapped through on the emulator (logically it goes through the merged ids, see above).
- The mock hub's linking fields are being added to `scripts/mock/mock_hub.py` by the iOS lane; until then a wrapper that doesn't modify it is used: like `android.sh shots`, it execs its source, replaces `identity()` (used by both health and hello), and adds a fixed `hub_id`, `hub_name` and `mesh`.
  Two groups: two mock hubs (one port each, no mesh), one paired with `debug.mockAddress`, the other with `--es debug.addMockAddress http://<loopback address>:<port>` (debug builds only, equivalent to **Add hub** in Settings). Two hubs in one group: two mock hubs whose mesh lists each other, only one paired; stop it and the connection switches to the other.

## SSH tunnel and local proxy (iOS parity)

A paired hub whose addresses don't answer can be reached through an SSH server or another app's local SOCKS5 proxy, as on
iOS ([network.md](../operations/network.md) "Connecting to hub through SSH" and "Connecting to hub through a local SOCKS5
proxy", where the Android notes are). The in-app tunnel is not ported: its core is a private library linked only into the
iOS app.

- **Settings › Hubs**: under each paired hub, an **SSH tunnel** and a **Local proxy** row ("· name" added when a group has
  several paired hubs), valued Not set / In use / Always / Fallback like iOS `HubsView`. Their pages
  (`android/feature/settings/src/main/kotlin/persona/feature/settings/HubTunnelScreens.kt`, models in `HubTunnelState.kt`)
  follow iOS `SSHTunnelView` and `LocalProxyView`: status and last failure, this device's public key with Copy public key /
  Copy authorized_keys line, the server, the tunnel target, the pinned fingerprint, Always, **Test connection and save**
  (a new or changed fingerprint is asked about first) and Delete. Switching Always alone on a saved tunnel saves at once.
  Diagnostics name tunnel candidates "SSH tunnel" and "Local proxy".
- **Connection**: one `HubWays` (`android/core/hub/src/main/kotlin/persona/core/hub/tunnel/HubWays.kt`) in
  `ConnectionRuntime` runs at most one tunnel of each kind per hub for every group connection. `OkHttpHubConnection`
  picks the way with `HubWay.next` (pure, the order iOS uses) and publishes the tunnel it goes through (`tunnel`), which
  `GroupRuntime` hands to Settings. A group's tunnels follow the registry: new settings reach the open connection
  (`GroupRuntime.updateTunnels`), an unpaired hub's tunnels close (`HubWays.retain`). Saving a tunnel's page clears a
  failure that waited for the user (`HubWays.unblock`) and redials a connection that is not online.
- **Tests**: `Socks5HandshakeTest`, `HubWayTest`, `LocalProxyForwardTest` (a SOCKS5 proxy on loopback),
  `SshTunnelTest` (a real SSH server, Apache MINA SSHD, test-only), `HubTunnelConnectionTest` (a group connection through
  each tunnel, Always, moving back once the address answers, a changed host key that is not redialled, a refused key
  dialled again after saving) in `core:hub`;
  `HubTunnelStateTest` and `HubTunnelShotsTest` (Hubs and both pages, empty, saved and failing, three styles × light /
  dark in Chinese plus English and Japanese, written to `build/tunnel-shots/`) in `feature:settings`.
- **Release**: R8 would remove every algorithm JSch makes by name (it did, before the rules), so `proguard-rules.pro`
  keeps them and `scripts/release/check_android_shrink.py` checks one of each kind ("Release shrinking" below).

## Build and install

```sh
./scripts/app/android.sh          # build the debug package, install it on the connected phone / emulator and open it
./scripts/app/android.sh apk      # APK only: android/app/build/outputs/apk/debug/app-debug.apk
./scripts/app/android.sh release  # R8 release build, checked, aligned and signed: android/app/build/outputs/apk/release/app-release.apk
./scripts/app/android.sh release -s <phone>   # and installed on that phone
./scripts/app/android.sh test     # unit tests for all modules (including the shared fixture entry)
./scripts/app/android.sh lint     # Android lint
```

- **Use `release` on the phone you actually use.** The debug package is debuggable, so ART never compiles it ahead of time, and it skips R8: it starts and scrolls noticeably worse ("Debug against release" below has the numbers). `release` runs `:app:assembleRelease`, checks the APK with `scripts/release/check_android_shrink.py`, then aligns and signs it with the newest build-tools. The key is `android.release_keystore` in `~/.config/persona/config.json` (with `android.release_key_alias` and `android.release_keystore_password_command`; [config.md](../operations/config.md)). With none configured it signs with the SDK's local debug keystore (`~/.android/debug.keystore`), which is what debug builds on the same computer are signed with, so the release installs over one of the same application id and keeps its pairing; the script says so when it does. That key is only for your own phone: its password is the same on every computer, so anyone can sign with a debug key; do not pass such a package on to someone else. A package signed on another computer does not update this one. Android refuses an update whose signature differs, so changing keys means uninstalling first, which removes the pairing. The published APKs are built and signed by the release worker ([release.md](../operations/release.md)), not by this command; a version tag's GitHub Release carries one too, `Persona-<version>-android.apk` with its `android.json` (release.md "Android on version tags"), so a phone running a debug-key build has to uninstall it once before it can take them.
- Needs the Android SDK (`$ANDROID_HOME`, default `~/Library/Android/sdk`) with the API 37 platform and matching build-tools installed; needs JDK 21 (Android Studio's bundled JBR 21 works).
- The toolchain is Gradle 9.8.2, AGP 9.5.0, Kotlin 2.4.21, Compose BOM 2026.09.00. Versions are centralized in `android/gradle/libs.versions.toml`; the wrapper and daemon configuration are committed too.
- AGP 9 uses built-in Kotlin and no longer applies `org.jetbrains.kotlin.android`. `build-logic/convention` provides `persona.android.application`, `persona.android.library`, `persona.jvm.library` and `persona.compose`, unifying SDK and JVM configuration; JVM bytecode is still 17.
- The script checks the launching JVM; when `JAVA_HOME` points to an old version such as JDK 8, it falls back to Android Studio's JBR. `gradle/gradle-daemon-jvm.properties` also pins the daemon to JDK 21, and the IDE should use the same version.
- `test` runs each module's unit tests through Gradle's task selector; `lint` only runs Android lint; ktlint / detekt are not adopted yet. The build cache and configuration cache are on.
- `app/lint-baseline.xml` only records two existing false positives about the notification permission: an `allowed()` check precedes the call, and the call itself is inside `runCatching`. The original notification code is kept; other errors still fail the build and existing warnings are still shown.
- Target is 37: the manifest declares Android 17's local network permission `ACCESS_LOCAL_NETWORK`, which is checked before pairing and connecting; when it's not granted the App explains and guides you to grant it (`app/.../platform/LocalNetworkPermission.kt`, `feature/settings/.../PairingLocalNetwork.kt`).
- The Application ID comes from configuration: `android.application_id` in `~/.config/persona/config.json`, falling back to `app.bundle_id`, then `com.example.persona`.
- To install two versions side by side on the same phone (e.g. to compare main line and the new version): call Gradle directly with a different `-PpersonaApplicationId` and app name `-PpersonaAppLabel` (default "Persona"), for example `./gradlew -PpersonaApplicationId=<package>.next -PpersonaAppLabel="Persona Next" :app:assembleDebug`. Both versions register the `pair` / `open` deep links for `persona://` and `persona://`, so when scanning a pairing code the system asks which App to open it with. Pairing deep links, QR scanning, "Paste link" and the manual input all accept both `persona://pair` and `persona://pair` (the check goes through `Pairing.isLink`); links generated by the hub are still `persona://` (see [rename.md](../operations/rename.md)). When streaming a debug package of about 90 MB over USB, some phones drop adb partway; installing once more is enough.
- Plaintext HTTP is only for local or trusted networks; on the public internet use HTTPS / WSS with pairing fingerprint pinning (pairings without a fingerprint are checked against system CAs, so the hub can sit under a reverse proxy subpath), preferring IPv6. The actual target is validated by `HubHttpClient`; see [network.md](../operations/network.md).
- The App icon is the same image as iOS (`apple/Persona/Resources/Assets.xcassets/AppIcon.appiconset/icon.png`, the mochi, #712): the adaptive icon's foreground places this image in the 72 dp safe zone in the middle of the 108 dp canvas,
  and the image's edge paper color extends outward to fill the whole canvas (`drawable-nodpi/ic_launcher_art.png`, 648 px; `drawable/ic_launcher_fg.xml` fills without inset), so whether the launcher crops it to a circle or rounded square, and during parallax motion, no seam shows;
  the background color `launcher_bg` is the image edge's beige `#E5D4C2`, visible only on very few launchers. The themed icon (Android 13+) uses iOS's tinted version, binarized, as a mask (`ic_launcher_mono_art.png`, a white silhouette with the eyes cut out).
  The status bar small icon `ic_stat` is a white silhouette of the mochi (two eyes cut out of the body, plus three waving lines; paths taken from the web's `MochiFace` / iOS `PersonaFace`, scaled from 1024 px coordinates to 24 dp), in `core/designsystem/src/main/res/drawable/`;
  every notification (ongoing, needs you, finished, phone tools, lock-screen test) and tile uses it. When iOS changes its icon, these two PNGs must be re-exported (as in #1136: `magick icon.png -resize 432x432 -virtual-pixel edge -set option:distort:viewport 648x648-108-108 -distort SRT 0`).
- Installing on a device: turn on USB debugging, connect the phone to the computer and run `./scripts/app/android.sh`, or send the APK to the phone and install it directly.

## Cache size budgets

`cacheBudgets = CacheBudgets(...)` in `HubDataStore` sets separate budgets, in bytes, for items, chat images and Inbox files. The defaults are 32 MiB, 128 MiB and 128 MiB respectively; these are an initial policy, **not measured**, and do not represent actual memory use or an assessment of device capacity.

- Items still keep the last two turns, strip the raw bytes of embedded images, and are charged by the UTF-8 byte count of the JSON in the database. A save measures only its own session (one query along the primary key) against per-session byte counts the cache keeps in memory; the scan of every cached session runs only while that total is unknown (the first open after the app starts, after a failed write) or over budget: when a save takes it over, and when a chat is opened or released while it is over (#4045). A save writes only the difference from the session's stored rows, in the same transaction: rows that are new or whose seq, part or JSON changed, and deletes of ids that left the window (writing the whole window again put every row in the WAL on each streaming update). Live items events coalesce per session: while one save waits, later events ride on it, and it reads the chat's latest state when it starts. Over budget, whole sessions are evicted by most recent read/write time, and their cursors are deleted too, so no false continuity record is left behind. A session is protected while a page subscribes to it; when protected data alone exceeds the budget, eviction is postponed and cleanup catches up once the last subscription is released. SQLite pages, indexes and the WAL are not counted against the JSON budget, and the database file may keep reusable free pages; this is not a hard cap on the database file's size.
- Chat images are downsampled proportionally to Coil's display size, orientation-corrected, encoded as PNG and written atomically into the pairing scope. The cache key includes the session, image id and target size, so an unsuitable thumbnail is never reused; without an explicit size the current screen size is used. Decoding and encoding run before the pairing's file lock is taken, so a chat's images don't wait for each other's encoding (#4047). Images are charged by their actual file bytes, a read only moves the file to the front of the LRU (writes and leaving a chat trim; reads never walk the directory), and all images of a visible session are protected. When a single image or protected files leave no room for a new image, the write is refused and the existing image failure notice reports it.
- Decoded chat images use one Coil loader per pairing data generation, with a 96 MiB memory cache. Chat pages borrow that loader, so leaving and reopening a chat keeps decoded images; each cache key starts with its own pairing scope. Removing a pairing or rebuilding its data shuts down the old loader and clears its memory cache. This budget is an initial policy, not a measured device-performance result.
- Inbox files are charged by the bytes actually written to disk; hits are reused directly, and over budget the least recently used files are evicted. File descriptors the system viewer opens through FileProvider hold a protecting reference, released on close; when a single file exceeds the budget, or open files fill the budget, the download returns an error. Attachments keep their original content; file downloads are not turned into thumbnails.

The budgets don't cover the outbox, drafts or persistent preferences. Inline Lark images keep using the existing in-memory cache of encoded bytes, with no new on-disk copy of originals. Peak image decoding, SQLite physical usage, cold start and scrolling performance are all unmeasured and still need acceptance on a real device.

## Inbox and assistant response memory

Chat message bodies synchronize only while their repository flow has an observer, for 60 seconds after it closes (the chat
stays subscribed, so switching tabs and back reopens it warm), and while the outbox waits to see a message sent from it in its
transcript. Session-list metadata still updates unread counts and status for other chats; reopening after the release verifies
the latest folded page before resuming. Four other idle chat entries retain warm navigation, keeping the most recently closed;
active observers remain protected. The continuity reducer reuses its sorted item list until the
transcript changes; duration metadata and realtime display rows also reuse unchanged inputs instead of rebuilding per
session-list event. Item aliases and live overlays invalidate the display projection whenever they change.

An inbox with a hub connection refreshes on inbox invalidations and reconnect, plus explicit page reads. Snapshot subscribers
include the shell and capsule, so their count only starts a slow five-minute poll that backs up missed events. Transports
without a hub event stream keep the 15-second polling fallback.

Unopened inbox conversations don't prefetch message bodies: the feature-side Lark window cache writes whole windows, and on
a real inbox three prefetched unread windows wrote a single 34 MB string and ran the process out of memory. Bulk "read FYI"
and exact unread counts therefore cover only conversations whose window has been opened. Window
snapshots are trimmed from their oldest messages to stay under 512 KiB when saved, and files larger than that (old versions
cached whole histories) are skipped before reading or decoding; such files are preserved but cannot provide offline first
paint. The latest page and older-history cursors remain available online. Rolling back this change restores the previous
cache-restore policy without a cache migration or data deletion.

Inbox/assistant JSON requests stream into the decoder on `Dispatchers.IO`, without first retaining a complete byte array and string. A process-wide permit covers request through decoding, so simultaneous repositories and pairing scopes cannot accumulate buffered responses while waiting to parse. Waiting callers are cancellable; decoding checks both coroutine cancellation and the current pairing before reading chunks and before returning a result.

The decoder rejects responses exceeding 4 MiB of decompressed JSON, 100,000 structural delimiters outside strings, or 128 nesting levels with `response_too_large`. The structural budget also bounds compact JSON that expands into many small objects. These are safety limits, not truncation or pagination: over-limit results are not partially applied, and existing repository data remains. Large endpoints still need server pagination to return additional data. Existing media and APK streaming behavior, authentication, TLS and pairing data are unchanged. A device crash stack identifies an allocation failure, not necessarily the largest retained object; large-response/concurrency regression tests do not replace device validation of the reported crash.

## Error reports (#5533)

The app's crashes and ANRs go to the hub's `error_reports` (format and rules: [protocol.md](../protocol/protocol.md) "Error reports"). The pure parts are in `:core:hub` (`persona.core.hub.errors`: report builder, disk queue, sender, exit-reason planner, ANR trace parser, breadcrumbs); `app/errors/ErrorReporting.kt` wires them into `PersonaApp`. There is no UI.

- **Crash**: an `UncaughtExceptionHandler`, installed first in `PersonaApp.onCreate`, writes the report synchronously (kind `crash`, level `fatal`, `previous_launch` true) and then hands the crash to the previous handler. It never throws; writing is one small file.
- **Next start**: `getHistoricalProcessExitReasons` turns `REASON_ANR` (kind `anr`, the main thread's Java frames parsed from the trace, nothing else from it) and `REASON_CRASH_NATIVE` into reports, each once (the newest exit timestamp seen is kept in the `error-reports` preferences). `REASON_CRASH` is reported only when the Java handler did not write it (no handler crash time within 10 s before the exit). The first run only records where it is: exits from before the reporter are not sent.
- **Content**: exception types, messages (`message` ≤ 1 KB), frames (class, method, source file name, line; `in_app` for `persona.*`), at most 5 causes × 64 frames, the release (`versionName`), build (`versionCode`), Android version and `Build.MODEL`. Breadcrumbs are the last 20 navigations through `AppNavigator` (route type names and tab names, never route arguments); pages that switch content without the navigator leave no breadcrumb.
- **Queue and sending**: one JSON file a report in `files/error-reports/`, at most 50, at most 7 days old. Sent only to the selected group's hub (the one the hub-level pages show), whenever its connection is online and its hello's `features` has `error_reports`; online without it, the queue is deleted. Batches of ≤ 20 reports and < 240 KB through the connection's authenticated route (same token and `X-Persona-Client`), on `Dispatchers.IO`. 2xx deletes the batch (and `{"enabled": false}` deletes the rest); 4xx drops it, except 408 / 429; 5xx or no answer keeps it for the next time.

## Release shrinking

The release build runs R8 with shrinking and optimization but without obfuscation (`android/app/build.gradle.kts`,
`android/app/proguard-rules.pro`, #2441, #2840): it removes the classes and members nothing reaches, then inlines,
merges and outlines code; with `-dontobfuscate` the classes and members that are left keep their source names. Resource
shrinking stays off (below), and debug builds are not shrunk.

- **Size**: at 0fabb8556 the release APK was 92,979,891 bytes, 6 dex files and 61,136 classes; shrunk it was 32,358,397
  bytes. At 02202d070 the shrunk APK is 33,222,269 bytes, 3 dex files, 33,625 classes and 179,195 method references;
  optimized it is 19,476,047 bytes (-41%), one dex file, 13,451 classes and 59,922 method references, and an update
  download, which the hub sends gzipped ([protocol.md](../protocol/protocol.md) "Android packages"), is about 10.3 MB
  instead of 33.2 MB. Most of the removed code is library code. The build's
  `app/build/outputs/mapping/release/usage.txt` lists every removed class and member. Optimizing costs build time:
  `:app:minifyReleaseWithR8 --rerun` took a median 130.7 s instead of 85.7 s (five alternating runs on a shared
  machine, paired difference +43.8 s).
- **Error reports** ("Error reports" above): exception types and breadcrumbs keep their source names. Class merging folded
  exceptions into other classes, `CoreTextException` and four of its subclasses and 25 library ones (a lateinit crash
  came out as `coil3.network.HttpException`), so `-keepnames class * extends java.lang.Throwable` keeps every
  Throwable's name, the libraries' too: reports, and messages that fall back to the simple name, print whatever was
  thrown. R8 keeps the InnerClass annotation that gives a nested exception its simple name only when it keeps the names
  of both the exception and its outer class (seven persona nested exceptions lost theirs), so the rules also keep those
  five outer classes' names. Library exceptions nested in a class that is no Throwable (11, such as
  `FrameworkSQLiteOpenHelper$OpenHelper$CallbackException`) report their simple name as `Outer$Inner`. All 45 route
  classes keep their names without a rule. Frames are another matter: an
  inlined method runs inside its caller, a merged class's code under the class it went into, and outlined or desugared
  code under R8's names (`$r8$lambda$<hash>`, `$$ExternalSyntheticApiModelOutline`, source file `R8$$SyntheticClass`),
  so a frame's class, method and line can differ from the source. `retrace` from the SDK command-line tools maps them
  back with the build's `mapping.txt`, which the release worker keeps gzipped next to the unsigned APK it belongs to
  (`mapping-<unsigned sha256>.txt.gz`, about 12 MB; [release.md](../operations/release.md)).
- **Reached by name**: R8 keeps what code references, the libraries' own rules (AndroidX, WorkManager, Room, Hilt,
  kotlinx.serialization) and the default `@JavascriptInterface` rule. What the app itself reaches only by name needs a
  rule in `proguard-rules.pro`; today that is `HubFailure.getStatus` / `getCode`, which the tasks store looks up on a
  failed call, and the SSH tunnel's algorithms, which JSch makes from class names in its config (its JCE and core
  classes, and Tink's X25519 and Ed25519 that `SshAlgorithms` names there). `scripts/release/check_android_shrink.py` checks a release APK for manifest components and App Startup
  initializers with their no-argument constructors, workers' `(Context, WorkerParameters)` constructors, Room's
  generated `_Impl` classes, the Markdown bridge's `done` / `fail`, those getters, one SSH algorithm of each kind, the InnerClass annotations that give
  persona exceptions and routes their simple names, an empty `missing_rules.txt`, every class R8's `seeds.txt` lists
  either in the dex or removed in `usage.txt`, never merged, and every Throwable in the dex among those seeds (the
  `-keepnames` rule matches them all). The release worker runs it before signing ([release.md](../operations/release.md) "Android publisher
  repository migration and status retries"):

  ```bash
  cd android && ./gradlew :app:assembleRelease
  python3 ../scripts/release/check_android_shrink.py app/build/outputs/apk/release/app-release-unsigned.apk \
    app/build/outputs/mapping/release
  ```

- **Rollback**: `-dontoptimize` in `proguard-rules.pro` goes back to shrinking only (a 33 MB APK; the name rules can
  stay). `isMinifyEnabled = false` in `android/app/build.gradle.kts` turns R8 off. Either way, change the assertion that
  pins it in `scripts/release/test_check_android_shrink.py` in the same commit. The test runs in the scripts CI shard and in the
  release worker before it builds, so a merge or an edit that turns shrinking off or drops a name rule by mistake stops
  there instead of publishing a 93 MB APK or renamed exceptions. After `isMinifyEnabled = false` the rules file is
  unused, and the check reads only the APK.
- **Compose trace markers**: optimized R8 already removes them (no `traceEventStart` call or trace string is left in the
  dex, against 1,528 trace strings in the shrunk APK), so `includeTraceMarkers = false` in the Compose compiler options
  saves nothing (the APK size did not change) and is not set.
- **Resource shrinking**: `isShrinkResources = true` takes 67,422 bytes off the optimized APK but removes 98 of
  persona's own resources from the table (strings and plurals that no code left after R8 references, such as
  `chat_composer_placeholder` and `notify_stopped_title`), so it stays off.
- **Icons**: icons come from `PhIcons` ([android-design.md](android-design.md)); `material-icons-extended`, whose only
  use was a button nothing called, is no longer a dependency. The debug APK went from 123,355,666 to 91,537,359 bytes
  (-31.8 MB, 29 to 28 dex files); R8 had already dropped the library from the release APK.

## Internationalization (简体中文 / English / 日本語, #2453)

Shared conventions (language list, fallback, glossary, protocol fields) are defined in `docs/protocol/i18n.md`; this section only covers how Android does it.

- **Languages and fallback**: `AppLanguage` (`core/contracts`) has four options: match system, 简体中文, English, 日本語. When matching the system, only the system's first preferred language counts (`docs/protocol/i18n.md`; it doesn't look further down): any Chinese (Traditional included) shows Simplified Chinese, Japanese shows Japanese, and everything else is English.
- **Switching**: Settings › This device › Language (`LanguageSettings.kt`). On Android 13 and above it writes the system's per-app language (`LocaleManager`, with the three languages registered in `res/xml/locales_config.xml`), so "App language" in system settings sees and changes the same value; on older systems it's stored in the App's own preferences and the Activity is `recreate()`d after switching. `AppCompatDelegate.setApplicationLocales` isn't used: the App's Activities are `ComponentActivity`, and below 13 AppCompat requires `AppCompatActivity` and an AppCompat theme, which costs more than applying the Configuration ourselves.
- **Applying it to the UI**: every Activity, `HubService` and the Application pin resources to the display language in `attachBaseContext` through `AppLanguages.wrap` (it only overrides the locale; light / dark and sizes still follow the system). This way a Traditional Chinese system also lands on `values-zh` (`zh-TW` would otherwise not match `values-zh` and fall through to English).
- **Resources**: `values/` is the English fallback, Chinese is in `values-zh/`, Japanese in `values-ja/`. English is the fallback because the fallback rule is "every other language shows English": with a French system Android takes `values/` directly; if `values/` were Chinese, French users would see Chinese, and places where the system reads resources directly without the App's Configuration (widgets, tiles, notification channel names) couldn't be fixed. `values-zh` has no region and covers `zh-CN`, `zh-Hans`, `zh-SG`. When packaging the App, `localeFilters` keeps only en / zh / ja, so libraries' bundled French and other translations don't leak into the English UI.
- **Keys**: all modules' resources merge into one table, so keys start with the module name (`settings_…`, `chat_…`), and each module keeps its own `strings.xml`. Language self-names ("简体中文", "English", "日本語") and the name Persona are marked `translatable="false"` and live only in `values/`. Plurals use `plurals` (English needs one / other, Chinese and Japanese only other).
- **Dates and numbers**: `LocalizedFormats` (`core/designsystem`) picks formats by skeleton (which fields to show: `MMMd`, `jmm`…), and the language decides order and wording; pages never write patterns containing words (`M月d日`). Inside Compose use `currentLocale`; outside Composables use `LocalizedFormats.locale(context)`.
- **Text from pure JVM modules**: `core/contracts`, `core/domain` and `core/hub` have no resources, so any human-facing text is returned as `CoreText` (`core/contracts`, a key plus arguments; lists and concatenation use `CoreText.joined`, user names and the hub's verbatim text use `CoreText.plain`), with entries in `res/values*/strings_core.xml` in `core/designsystem` (`designsystem_core_…`). Pages call `text()`, places with a Context call `resolve(context)`; places with no resources at hand (pure notification functions, exception messages, text that `core/data` repositories put into state) use `shown`, which goes through `CoreText.words`, pointed at the App's own resources in `PersonaApp.onCreate` at startup. Failures use `CoreTextException` (its `message` is the text in the current language). Unit tests: Android modules have `values-zh/strings_core.xml` on the test classpath (build-logic), `shown` reads it, and expected Chinese is written as is; JVM modules' tests read the same file through their own `zh()` helper. Demo mode data comes in three copies per language: `Demo.json` (Chinese), `Demo.en.json` and `Demo.ja.json` in `core/demo`, picked by the current language when entering demo mode.
- **Passing it to the hub**: `ClientLanguage` (`core/contracts`) holds the language currently displayed (`zh-Hans` / `en` / `ja`); the hub client sends `Accept-Language` on every request and WebSocket upgrade, and `client_hello` carries `locale`; when the language changes, `client_hello` is sent again on the current connection (`docs/protocol/i18n.md` ("Protocol")). Android doesn't register push devices, so there's no device `locale`.
- **Tests**: Robolectric tests run in Simplified Chinese by default (`android/build-logic/robolectric/robolectric.properties`; tests that set `qualifiers` must include the `zh-` prefix); to test other languages use `@Config(qualifiers = "en-…")` / `"ja-…"`; `LanguageShotsTest` is an example.
- **Checks** (no Android SDK needed; also run in CI's Privacy and workflow checks):

```sh
python3 scripts/app/android_i18n_check.py            # the three sets agree on keys / placeholders / plurals, key prefixes, no Chinese or Japanese in values/, hard-coded Chinese / Japanese and English UI text don't exceed the baselines
python3 scripts/app/android_i18n_check.py --update   # record the lower count after moving copy into strings.xml
(cd scripts/app && python3 test_android_i18n_check.py)
```

  Each module's `i18n-baseline.json` records its hardcoded text counts so modules can migrate independently; `i18n-english-baseline.json` does the same for English literals in Compose text positions (`Text("…")`, `contentDescription`, a composable's `title` / `label` / `text` … String parameter), and the check lists every recorded line. Counts can only decrease; the goal is to remove every baseline. Tests and the design system's developer catalog at `core/designsystem/src/debug/kotlin/persona/core/designsystem/catalog/` are excluded. Other `src/debug` client code and XML are checked normally; debug source sets must not bypass localization checks. Non-UI literals (hub text that must match, prompts sent to a model) explain their purpose on the same line with `// i18n-ignore: <reason>`.

## What the main chat is still missing (#4032)

Follows sections 4.4–4.6 of the new-user onboarding plan ([onboarding.md](../assistant/onboarding.md)). When the hub has `assistant_readiness`, the App reads `GET /api/v1/assistant/readiness` (`AssistantManagement.readiness`; the settings page goes through `AssistantReadinessSource`, against the selected group's hub); with an old hub's 404 everything stays as before.

- **Persona's blank page** (New chat's, or the one that starts the main chat; `MainReadiness` in `feature/assistant`): reloads on entering the page, coming back to the foreground, a computer going online / offline, and after opening the main chat is refused. On `setup`, a card above the composer (`ReadinessStepCard`, in the design system) shows only the first missing step; the send button becomes **Add a computer first / Wake the computer first / Confirm the computer first / Connect a model first** (`BlockedSendButton`), which does the same as the card's main button; the draft stays and nothing is queued. Example questions are replaced by the hub's `suggestions`; `local` ones open an explanation (`ReadinessHelpDialog`) and aren't sent to the model; the subtitle becomes "one step left before you can start". `chat_only` is a single hint plus **Add a computer**.
- **Where buttons go**: `connect_model` → Settings › Model providers; `edit_provider` → that provider (when there's a `launcher`); `add_computer` → Machines › Add a computer; `confirm_computer` → confirm that computer (#4297, see "Onboarding: model, devices, computers" below; the full-page checklist after pairing has no navigation, so `add_computer` there is still an explanation dialog); `wake_computer`, `install_claude` and `login_claude` are explanation dialogs; `test_assistant` runs `?probe=1` and writes the result on the card. Because the full-page checklist after pairing has no navigation, its explanation dialogs don't offer **Connect a model** or **Use an API key** (tapping would only reopen the same explanation), only **Got it**.
- **Opening the main chat is refused**: `OutboxMessageSender` recognizes a create failure that is a 409 with one of the codes above (`SetupFailureCodes`), marks that message as refused (`error` is the code) and hands back the "create main chat" placeholder, so **Retry** after setup creates it again; other failures still only look up the main chat pointer (it may already exist). A card under the message (`AssistantPendingConversation`) gives the reason, the button for that step and **Retry**. When creation hits `node_unreachable` / `assistant_offline` (computer asleep, assistant restarting) it also hands back the placeholder; while the hub link is up the message is tried again every 10 s (each try checks the pointer first, then creates), and after 60 s it is shown as not sent, with that reason when the hub gave one, and **Retry**. Every refused message in this queue gets that card, not only setup refusals (#2821).
- **Coded feedback**: `mapOperationFailure` recognizes `no_machine`, `machine_pending`, `node_unreachable`, `claude_missing`, `launcher_missing`, `not_logged_in`, `auth_failed` and `assistant_offline`; `operationFeedback` supplies localized text and the appropriate action. `node_unreachable` says the computer is offline, not that the hub is unreachable. The install and login actions match the hub's `action`. Pages display `OperationFeedback.text` from resources in the App's language; code outside Compose must explicitly resolve it with current resources too. There is no fixed-language `message` fallback. The hub-worded codes (`apierr.codeActions`: `no_machine`, `machine_pending`, `claude_missing`, `launcher_missing`) retain the hub's sentence only in `hubWords` for reports. Other codes can carry raw details such as `rpc: connection closed`, which are never displayed.
- **Setup checklist** (Settings › Checklist, and the full-page checklist after pairing): the phone items are preceded by four **Main chat** items (computer, Claude Code, model, first message), with state, the hub's reason and buttons; with these four present, the node / herdr version of each computer is no longer listed. Tapping **Skip the rest** (later) while the main chat isn't ready also sends `POST …/readiness/dismiss`, and the request isn't cancelled when the page closes; tapping **Done** sends nothing. An item the system can't read has **Mark done**, so **Done** appears once on the page.
- **First screen and empty states**: pairing's **Connect your hub** no longer shows `personad hub pair`; that's folded into **Other ways**. The copy for an expired pairing code / mismatched certificate becomes "generate a new code on a device that's signed in". The Machines page and Agents with zero computers show **No computers yet** + **Add a computer** (from #4297 it opens the Add a computer page), and the Agents empty state no longer mentions herdr.

## Onboarding: model, devices, computers (#4297)

Item 16 of `docs/assistant/onboarding.md` 7.2, built after the web (`ProviderWizard.tsx`, `ConnectDevice.tsx`, `AddComputer.tsx`).

- **New model provider**: when the hub's hello has `builtin_presets` (`ProviderSetup.builtinPresets`) it's a wizard (`NewProviderScreen.kt` + `ProviderWizardSteps.kt`, logic in `NewProviderModel`): ① lists the hub's presets (built-in ones with a description, extension ones labeled **Extension** with their address), with **Custom compatible gateway** last; ② just paste the key: after a 0.7-second pause, with length at least 8, it automatically sends `POST /api/v1/launchers/test` (with `preset` and the preset's `auth`; no `model` unless the model was changed; a result superseded by a later edit is dropped; Messages, Responses and Chat Completions presets are tested (an empty protocol means Messages)); **Where do I get a key?** opens `signup_url`; **Use it for the main conversation and new Agents** follows readiness (off by default when computer, Claude Code and model are all `ok`), and saving first waits for the first readiness response before deciding whether to send `assign`; a Responses or Chat Completions direct API instead hides that switch, omits `assign`, does not wait for readiness and skips the Claude Code warning, and shows the direct-API note on ② and ③; the name (left empty, the hub names it) and default model are folded into **More options**; ③ writes "Now used by …" and the reason for `kept`, and if readiness says Claude Code is missing before or after saving, it prompts to install it (`ReadinessHelp.InstallClaude`). Only **Done** goes back to the previous page (`onSaved` receives the name the hub chose). The custom gateway is the original form, plus the auth method (x-api-key / Bearer) and **Test connection** / **Fetch models** (`/launchers/test`, `/launchers/discover`; fetched models fill an empty default model, and **Pick one** changes it). Old hubs (without `builtin_presets`) still get **Fill in / From extension**, and the form also has test and fetch models.
- **Starting from what you already have** (#5220): with `launcher_import_parse` (`ProviderSetup.launcherImportParse`), the choices above are followed by **Paste a configuration** and, only when there is one, **Found on your computers**; an older hub shows neither. The paste page (`ProviderPasteScreen.kt`, state in `NewProviderModel` and pure mapping in `ProviderPasteState.kt`) reads the text after the same 0.7-second pause as the web, immediately for a multi-character insertion, drops late answers and answers for older text, and shows every parser note and kept / skipped variable or header by name, never by value. **Continue** reuses the existing form: a new draft prefills its connection, models, tiers and advanced fields; a same-key match opens the stored provider with only new models added, and another-key match with only its key changed. Parsing saves nothing. Found aliases follow the web filter (zshrc, an address, not hidden, no managed `zshrc:<alias>@<machine>` origin on any of their computers) and open `ProviderImportPage` with a fixed `machine` + `alias` on the first online computer (the first when none is online); preview and POST both carry `alias`, while the ordinary full-machine import stays unchanged. Protocol and parsing rules remain in [launchers.md](../integrations/launchers.md) and [model-config.md](../assistant/model-config.md); regression and three-style light / dark capture entry points are `ProviderPasteStateTest`, `ProviderPasteNotesTest`, `ProviderPasteShotsTest`, `HttpProviderSetupTest` and `HttpProviderListTest`.
- **Settings › Devices › Connect a new device** (`ConnectDevice.kt`): first records the existing device credentials, then `POST /api/v1/auth/pair-codes {"web": <the address this phone uses for the hub>/}`; two targets, **Persona App / Browser**, drawing the hub's `qr` / `web_qr` (`JQrCode`); copying the link (marked sensitive on Android 13+, so it's kept out of the clipboard preview); a per-second countdown and reading the device list every 3 seconds, where the extra device is the one that used the code; it regenerates on expiry, and after connecting offers **Connect another**. When the phone reaches the hub through local forwarding (a loopback address), it warns that the browser link won't open elsewhere; when an old hub lacks `web_url` it says to upgrade first.
- **Machines › Add a computer** (`AddComputerRoute`, `AddComputerPage.kt` / `AddComputerModel.kt`): choose the system and a name (up to 40 characters) → `pair-codes {"add"}` (selected group) → steps per system (Linux installs in one line when there's `install.linux`, otherwise `persona-link pair`; Windows is one PowerShell line; Mac installs Persona Link + copy / share the link) → wait for it to connect (the WebSocket machine list, plus `GET /api/v1/machines` every 3 seconds, continuing while asking whether it is yours) → on the same screen **It's mine / No, remove** (`…/confirm` / `…/reject`). On expiry it issues a new code (`{"machine"}`), unless the computer has already been online since the code was made (or the hub reports an error other than `never_connected`): offline then shows the offline state with no new code, since a new code revokes its pairing (#5307), and polling continues so it returns to the question when it wakes; seen and then gone means it was deleted. `AddComputerRoute(confirm)` confirms a computer: `""` takes the first online computer pending confirmation; offline ones get **Remove** and **New link**. Error codes (`tls_required`, `mesh_member`, `too_many_pending`, `forbidden`, `invalid_name`) have their own text; for the assistant asked for (`no_manager`, `installing`, `assistant_offline`, `owners_unknown`) the hub's own words show. On `too_many_pending` the computers in the way are listed under the form from the selected hub's whole list (every assistant's, not only the current one's, which the Computers page shows), each with how it stands (never connected, waiting for you, offline) and **Remove** (`…/reject`), as the web lists them. **The current assistant's** (several assistants, [managers.md](../assistant/managers.md) "Clients"): "Next" sends the current assistant's id as `add.manager` (the default's real id too; before the roster is read, the pick this device kept for the hub (or the default it last read, when that is not the hub's own), and none with neither, and none when the selected group's hub is not the one the current assistant is on); a new code for a computer (`{"machine"}`) names none. When the reply's `manager` is another one, or missing (a hub from before `add.manager` puts a new computer under its own assistant), the waiting page says "This computer was added under <name>." (`placedUnder`, as the web's `addedUnder`: a missing `manager` means the hub's own assistant for a computer it added and the machines list's for one it reused; nothing when that is the one asked for, nor with one assistant). While the current assistant is offline the form says "<name> is offline, so a computer can't be added to it right now." and Next is off. When the hub lacks `machines_add`, the page says to add it from the web or the admin interface. The interface is `ComputerPairing` (`core:contracts`), `HttpComputerPairing` (`core:hub`), DI in `MachineManagementModule.computers`: adding a computer uses the selected group; the rest go to the group named by the machine id's suffix and use its own id.
- **Machine list**: cards with `trust: "pending"` are labeled **To confirm**, say it's waiting for confirmation, and have **Confirm computer**; when offline, known `error_code`s (`node_service_down`, `link_offline`, `never_connected`, `node_outdated`) become plain words, and unknown ones still show `error`. The "+" at the top right of the overview is Add a computer.
- Unit tests: `OnboardingWireTest` in `core:hub`; `ProviderWizardTest` and `ConnectDeviceModelTest` in `feature:settings`; `AddComputerModelTest`, `AddComputerAssistantTest` (the current assistant's id sent, where the hub put it, an offline assistant, the computers in the way) in `feature:machines`; `HttpComputerPairingTest` in `core:hub` (`add.manager` sent, the reply's `manager`, an older hub's reply without it, the refusals). Screenshots (Robolectric, three styles × light / dark, written to each module's `build/onboarding-shots/`): `OnboardingSettingsShotsTest`, `AddComputerShotsTest`, `AddComputerAssistantShotsTest` (the waiting page saying where the computer went, an offline assistant's form, the computers in the way; Glass, Chinese light and dark, English and Japanese).

Screenshots: `AssistantReadinessShotsTest`, `ReadinessMainShotsTest`, `PairingOtherWaysTest` (Robolectric, three styles × light / dark, written to each module's `build/onboarding-shots/`).

GitHub star prompts (#5761, `docs/design/DESIGN_SYSTEM.md` "GitHub star prompts"): the readiness screen's **Done** owes the onboarding toast, which `StarPromptWelcome` shows from the root's toast host once the shell is up, so leaving the checklist (or a relaunch that drew it outside the shell) keeps it; the Agents list puts the pill under its first-run empty state and the milestone card in the hub notice's slot (`StarPrompts.kt`), and each fresh start from New Agent counts. The rules are core:domain's `StarPrompt.kt` (`StarPromptFixtureTest` replays `spec/fixtures/star-prompt`); the state is `StarPromptStore` in device-wide preferences (`persona-star-prompt`) that unpairing keeps. Tests and pictures: `StarPromptStoreTest`, `NewAgentStarCountTest`, `StarPromptShotsTest` (three styles × light / dark, `build/star-prompt-shots/`), `StarPromptWelcomeTest`.

## Design token adoption baseline

Run the standalone check from the repository root; no Android SDK needed:

```sh
python3 scripts/app/android_token_baseline.py
python3 scripts/app/android_token_baseline.py --report /tmp/android-token-report.json
python3 scripts/app/test_android_token_baseline.py
```

The baseline is stored in `android/core/designsystem/tools/token-baseline.json`: each file's nonzero hard-coded counts, nothing else (`--report` writes the full counts). The script scans production Kotlin in every module (`android/app`, `android/core/*`, `android/feature/*`, `android/platform/*`; #4209), the design system included, but not the generated `Tokens.kt`, whose literals are the tokens and which `scripts/assets/gen_tokens.py --check` guards. It skips test directories, ignores comments, strings and imports, and recognizes explicit imports and aliases. It counts hard-coded values, token references and adoption rates per module for colors, font sizes and dp; adoption is `token / (token + hard-coded)`, or `null` when there is no usage.

Colors include numeric `Color(...)`, named constants and `parseColor(...)`; font sizes include numeric `.sp`; dp includes all numeric `.dp`, including spacing, sizes and radii. Tokens include the corresponding `Tokens`, `PersonaTheme.colors`, `PersonaTypography` and theme-mapped `MaterialTheme` references. These are source-reference counts, not runtime style coverage; variable propagation, wildcard member imports, expressions inside string templates and custom wrappers are not fully analyzed semantically.

Since the 2026-10-31 redesign, `PersonaTheme.palette` / `type` / `shapes` also count as tokens; feature colors and font sizes all use tokens, while spacing between page elements still uses numeric dp (each style's internal padding lives in design system components). The baseline was updated once against the redesigned code.

A fifth category, `icon`, counts the Phosphor glyphs pages name directly (`PhIcons.*`, outside `android/core/designsystem`); `PersonaIcons.*`, the icons by meaning, count as its tokens, so moving a page to meaning names lowers it (#4209).

A fourth category, `material3`, counts the Material 3 components pages may not use ([android-design.md](android-design.md) "2.3 Rules for writing pages"): calls to a denied component imported from `androidx.compose.material3`, or written out in full, outside `android/core/designsystem`, which wraps them. `Text`, `MaterialTheme`, `Local*` and `material3.adaptive` are not counted. A wildcard `material3` import outside the design system fails the check outright, with no baseline, since it hides which components a page uses.

Hard-coded counts for each file and category must not exceed the baseline; new files start at zero. Existing code with permitted fixed sizes, such as the terminal, also counts toward the baseline, and new usage is checked too. Reducing hard-coded values in another file does not offset additions in this file. When a change really must be accepted, run `python3 scripts/app/android_token_baseline.py --update-baseline` and review the baseline diff; the normal check never rewrites it. After merging several lanes, run the check first, fix new hard-coded values first, then decide whether to update the baseline. The Swift output is out of scope here.

## Design-system slot order

A component in `core/designsystem` with a composable slot (`@Composable … -> …`) ends with a function-typed parameter, so callers can pass the slot as a trailing lambda (`JSectionHeader(title) { … }`); a new parameter goes before the slots (#4146). A parameter added after the slot broke `main` in #4124 (#4145). Check it from the repository root; no Android SDK needed:

```sh
python3 scripts/app/android_slot_order.py
python3 scripts/app/test_android_slot_order.py
```

The script reads the non-private `@Composable fun` components under `android/core/designsystem/src/main`, ignoring comments and strings. Components that already end otherwise are listed in `android/core/designsystem/tools/slot-order-baseline.json` with a count per file and name (overloads counted together). That list may only shrink: `--update-baseline` rewrites it once a component keeps a slot last, and a new entry needs the same review as raising the token baseline. CI runs both beside the token checks (`scripts/ci/android.sh`).

## Modules and code

Modules are defined by `android/settings.gradle.kts`, and direct in-repository dependencies by each module's `build.gradle.kts`. Arrows below point from the consumer to its dependency; only production `project(...)` dependencies are listed, not external libraries or test dependencies.

| Module | Responsibility | Direct dependencies |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `:app` | App entry, two primary destinations and shared sidebar, Hilt assembly, pairing lifecycle, scope bridging, system sharing and lock-screen test | `:core:contracts`, `:core:domain`, `:core:terminal`, `:core:navigation`, `:core:hub`, `:core:data`, `:core:chatlist`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:demo`, `:feature:chat`, `:feature:agents`, `:feature:assistant`, `:feature:inbox`, `:feature:settings`, `:feature:terminal`, `:feature:machines`, `:feature:files`, `:feature:knowledge`, `:feature:voice`, `:platform:notify`, `:platform:phonetools`, `:platform:shortcuts`, `:platform:capsule` |
| `:core:chatlist` | Inverted chat list, freezing, return to latest, reading anchors and paging effects; currently mainly used by Agent / assistant chats | No in-repository module dependencies |
| `:core:contracts` | Pure JVM: wire models, event decoding, HubScope, repository and transport interfaces | No in-repository module dependencies |
| `:core:data` | Room items / outbox / drafts, DataStore / Tink credentials, scopes, caches and real repositories | `:core:contracts`, `:core:domain`, `:feature:inbox` |
| `:core:demo` | Local demo data and DemoHub, using sample fixtures in assets | `:core:contracts`, `:core:hub` |
| `:core:designsystem` | Tokens for three styles (palettes, typography, shapes, motion), component skins (page scaffolds, lists, buttons, decisions, chats, Inbox), shell navigation components, glass (Haze); bundled fonts (OFL) and Phosphor icons | `:core:domain`, `:core:hub` |
| `:core:domain` | Pure JVM: continuity reducer, chatLines (and `ChatLinesMemo`, the same lines keeping the turns before the first change between calls), sorting, Inbox sections, answering policies and pairing parsing | `:core:contracts` |
| `:core:hub` | Pure JVM: OkHttp HTTP / WS, TLS pinning, routing, heartbeat, pairing and business transports; error reports (see "Error reports") | `:core:contracts` |
| `:core:navigation` | Navigation 3 route types, Navigator, notification and machine page routes | No in-repository module dependencies |
| `:core:terminal` | Pure JVM: VT grid, Unicode width, wrapping and terminal input encoding | No in-repository module dependencies |
| `:feature:agents` | Computer → space → session list, request cards, create and close confirmation, space page, a computer's projects page (`MachineSpacesRoute`, opened from the space page's computer), session and space renaming (immediate local effect) | `:core:contracts`, `:feature:chat`, `:core:domain`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:assistant` | Home, history drawer, main chat sending, profile and scheduled task pages; management services assembled by the App's `ChatWiringModule`; dedicated chat styling still awaits wiring | `:core:contracts`, `:core:data`, `:core:domain`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation`, `:feature:chat` |
| `:feature:chat` | Shared chat, Markdown, tools / diffs / to-dos, photo and file tags, long-press menu, error cards, image viewing and markup, interaction panel, attachment components and in-app link reader | `:core:contracts`, `:core:domain`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:chatlist`, `:core:navigation`, `:feature:inbox` |
| `:feature:files` | File tree, text / image / Markdown / PDF preview, Git and commit details, path insertion; entered from the Agent chat ⋯ menu and file tags in messages | `:core:contracts`, `:core:hub`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:inbox` | Unified Inbox, Lark chats, mail details, AI actions and reply components | `:core:chatlist`, `:core:contracts`, `:core:hub`, `:core:domain`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:knowledge` | Knowledge base list, reading and navigation; entered from the Persona drawer | `:core:contracts`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:machines` | Machine dashboard, details and metric charts; entered from the Agents machine menu | `:core:contracts`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:domain`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:settings` | Pairing, checklist, diagnostics, devices and notification preferences, About and APK installation | `:core:contracts`, `:core:domain`, `:core:hub`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:terminal` | Terminal Canvas, streaming frame consumption, takeover and input; entered from the chat page and the notification terminal fallback | `:core:contracts`, `:core:terminal`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:feature:voice` | Full-duplex voice chat with the hub's voice extension; see "Voice chat" | `:core:contracts`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:platform:notify` | Foreground service, ongoing summary notification (`FleetFeed`), notification policy and buttons, action log, boot receiver and connection keep-alive | `:core:contracts`, `:core:domain`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation` |
| `:platform:phonetools` | Phone tool catalog, registration, permissions and write confirmation, contacts / calendar / photos / location implementations; entered from Settings › Phone data | `:core:contracts`, `:core:navigation`, `:core:designsystem` |
| `:platform:shortcuts` | Home-screen widgets (Glance), Quick Settings tiles, icon long-press shortcuts and "Approve pending" dialog; see "Ongoing notification and home-screen entries" | `:core:contracts`, `:core:domain`, `:core:designsystem`, `:core:navigation`, `:core:hub`, `:platform:notify` |
| `:platform:capsule` | Cross-app floating capsule (`TYPE_APPLICATION_OVERLAY` overlay); see "Cross-app floating capsule" | `:core:contracts`, `:core:designsystem` |

The main dependency direction is `:app → feature / platform / core`. Features consume interfaces from `:core:contracts`, and the App's Hilt modules assemble real implementations. Cross-layer dependencies still exist: `:core:data → :feature:inbox`, `:feature:assistant → :core:data`, and between features `:feature:agents → :feature:chat`, `:feature:assistant → :feature:chat`, `:feature:chat → :feature:inbox`; `:feature:files`, `:feature:inbox` and `:feature:settings` also use `:core:hub` directly. This is the current build graph, not yet fully aligned with the designed one-way layering. `:core:domain → :core:contracts`, `:core:hub → :core:contracts`; `:core:terminal` depends on no other project module.

`build-logic/convention` is included via `includeBuild("build-logic")` and is not an app module. It provides application, library, JVM, Compose and Hilt convention plugins; Hilt is integrated, while Room / KSP, DataStore and Tink live in the data module. Versions are in `gradle/libs.versions.toml`.

`app/di/ConnectionRuntime.kt` owns the connection and pairing scope; `ChatDataModule` and `InboxModule` assemble real repositories. HTTP and WS share the current candidate and credentials; re-pairing switches the entire `HubScope(hub_id, pairingGeneration)`, closes old coroutines and deletes old scope data. Agent and assistant composer drafts are saved in the current pairing's `DraftStore` (`ChatWiringModule` hands `data.drafts` to both).

### Build, tests and screenshots

Run from the repository root:

```sh
./scripts/app/android.sh install  # same as no arguments: build, install and open
./scripts/app/android.sh apk      # build the debug APK only
./scripts/app/android.sh test     # unit tests in each module
./scripts/app/android.sh lint     # Android lint
```

The script supports `install|apk|test|lint|shots` (for `shots`, see "Batch screenshots with a mock hub" below), and does not forward arbitrary Gradle tasks. To verify only relevant modules, call the wrapper directly, for example:

```sh
cd android
./gradlew :core:domain:test :core:hub:test
./gradlew :feature:chat:testDebugUnitTest
```

`core:designsystem` has pixel-checking component unit tests (`EnabledStateTest`) using Robolectric native rendering (`@GraphicsMode(NATIVE)`); the first run downloads Robolectric's Android runtime (SDK 35) from Maven Central. Shared specifications live in `spec/fixtures/`; relevant tests read continuity, lines, answers, sorting, Inbox and terminal fixtures. The convention plugin passes the repository root, which can also be set with `-PpersonaRepoRoot=<repository root>`. These commands were not run for this page; listing them does not claim tests passed.

Screenshots use a dedicated emulator, a mock hub and adb; first run `python3 scripts/mock/mock_hub.py` in a separate terminal, then run the commands below. The package name matches the configuration of `scripts/app/android.sh`; replace placeholders and do not connect to a production hub.

```sh
./scripts/app/android.sh apk
export ANDROID_SERIAL='<dedicated emulator serial>'
PERSONA_PACKAGE='<application_id from configuration>'
ADB="${ANDROID_HOME}/platform-tools/adb"
"$ADB" install -r android/app/build/outputs/apk/debug/app-debug.apk
"$ADB" reverse tcp:18760 tcp:18760
"$ADB" shell "am start -a android.intent.action.VIEW -d 'persona://pair?url=http%3A%2F%2Flocalhost%3A18760&token=mock' $PERSONA_PACKAGE"
```

Confirm the connection on the pairing page, handle permissions and the checklist, then switch to the page to inspect. Take one screenshot in each of light and dark; for a direct session jump, use machine and session IDs from the mock hub:

```sh
"$ADB" shell cmd uimode night no
"$ADB" shell am force-stop "$PERSONA_PACKAGE"
"$ADB" shell "am start -a android.intent.action.VIEW -d 'persona://open?machine=<sample machine ID>&session=<sample session ID>' $PERSONA_PACKAGE"
"$ADB" exec-out screencap -p > /tmp/persona-android-light.png
"$ADB" shell cmd uimode night yes
"$ADB" shell am force-stop "$PERSONA_PACKAGE"
"$ADB" shell "am start -a android.intent.action.VIEW -d 'persona://open?machine=<sample machine ID>&session=<sample session ID>' $PERSONA_PACKAGE"
"$ADB" exec-out screencap -p > /tmp/persona-android-dark.png
```

Wait until the page is ready before taking screenshots. For batch screenshots use `./scripts/app/android.sh shots`; debug direct-entry arguments are below. None connect to a production hub. For scrolling performance see "Interface style performance".

## Debug against release

A debug package is for development: it is debuggable, so ART never compiles it ahead of time, and it skips R8. On the phone
you actually use, install a release build (`./scripts/app/android.sh release`, "Build and install" above).

Measured 2026-11-11 at `a5d75efcd` with `scripts/diag/android_build_perf.py`: the debug and the release package of that
commit installed side by side under two application ids, both paired with the same mock hub
(`MOCK_AGENTS=1 MOCK_LONG_HISTORY=1`, about 140 sessions), Glass style, the frame-drop fallback left on. Each round
opens the page with a `persona://open` link, waits 3.5 seconds, resets `dumpsys gfxinfo`, flings up and down twice and
reads the summary; 10 rounds per scenario, the two packages alternating inside each round. Frame times come from
gfxinfo's histogram, which counts every frame since the reset (1 ms buckets up to 31 ms, wider ones above). Janky is gfxinfo's own
"Janky frames ÷ Total frames" summed over the 10 rounds, with the per-round min / median / max in parentheses. The chat is
the long Agent conversation `MOCK_LONG_HISTORY=1` adds.

| Phone | Scenario | Build | Frames | p50 | p90 | p99 (ms) | 34 ms or longer | Janky |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Huawei BLK-AL80, HarmonyOS 4.2 (Android 12 base), 120 Hz panel | Agents list | debug | 2,584 | 15 | 34 | 105 | 10.10% | 11.57% (6.8 / 11.0 / 19.2) |
| | | release | 2,840 | 20 | 21 | 27 | 0.04% | 0.11% (0 / 0 / 0.7) |
| | Chat | debug | 2,517 | 16 | 38 | 121 | 11.08% | 12.16% (8.9 / 10.9 / 17.3) |
| | | release | 2,840 | 20 | 21 | 26 | 0% | 0% (0 / 0 / 0) |
| Xiaomi 12 (2201123C), Android 13, 120 Hz panel | Agents list | debug | 5,834 | 9 | 13 | 30 | 0.48% | 4.83% (4.3 / 4.8 / 5.5) |
| | | release | 5,943 | 17 | 17 | 20 | 0% | 1.51% (0 / 1.3 / 3.7) |
| | Chat | debug | 5,849 | 9 | 13 | 30 | 0.56% | 4.63% (3.7 / 4.5 / 5.8) |
| | | release | 5,965 | 16 | 17 | 21 | 0% | 1.83% (0.3 / 2.1 / 2.5) |

| Phone | Cold start, debug | Cold start, release |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Huawei BLK-AL80 | 2,431 ms (2,405 to 2,489) | 580 ms (552 to 617) |
| Xiaomi 12 | 851 ms (828 to 874) | 391 ms (376 to 406) |

Cold start is the median `TotalTime` of six `am start -W` runs after a force-stop, with the range in parentheses; the
packages were paired and had been opened before. APK size: 97.3 MB debug, 20.8 MB release.

Both panels are 120 Hz, but the Huawei phone drew about half as many frames per round as the Xiaomi phone in the same
steps, so it was most likely running at 60 Hz; the refresh rate in use was not recorded. Each build is compared with
the other on the same phone, not across phones.

- The long frames are the difference. On the Huawei phone one debug frame in ten takes 34 ms or longer and the 99th
  percentile is over 100 ms; the release build has almost none and its 99th percentile is under 30 ms. On the faster
  Xiaomi phone the debug build's long frames are fewer, and the release build still has none that long and well under
  half the janky share.
- The release build's median is not lower: its histogram puts most frames in the 16 to 21 ms buckets on both phones,
  where the debug build has a faster median and a long tail. Why the release frames sit there was not looked into, and
  it moves between runs: an earlier run on the Xiaomi phone put the release median at 8 ms for the Agents list. Read the
  tail and the janky share, not the median.
- These are two phones, one run each. An earlier run of the same steps agreed: on the Huawei phone 11.33% against
  1.62% janky for the Agents list and 10.58% against 0% for the chat. The release build has no Baseline Profile of the
  app's own yet, only the one merged from its libraries (#4055), so its first launches after an install are the slowest
  it gets.

`android_scroll_perf.py` (below) opens pages through the debug entry and only runs on a debug package. To compare
builds, install them under different application ids (`-PpersonaApplicationId`, "Build and install"), pair each one,
and run:

```sh
CHAT=<machine>/<session> COLD=6 python3 scripts/diag/android_build_perf.py <serial> out.json debug=<package> release=<package>
```

## Interface style performance

Measured 2026-10-31 on a test phone (Android, ColorOS, connected to a real hub) with `scripts/diag/android_scroll_perf.py`: debug package, pages opened through debug entries
(`debug.style` switches style, `debug.automation` disables dropped-frame fallback so the measurement captures the cost of glass itself), wait 3.5 seconds, reset `dumpsys gfxinfo`, swipe twice in each direction,
then read framestats. Frame time = `FrameCompleted − IntendedVsync`; each run takes the last roughly 120 frames in framestats (unfinished and invalid rows removed), 10 runs per scenario.
Jank rate is gfxinfo's own "Janky frames ÷ Total frames", summed across 10 runs; parentheses show per-run min / p50 / max.
`RUNS`, `SWIPE_MS` and `SETTLE_S` override the run count, swipe duration and idle delay. `SWIPE_MS=1000 SETTLE_S=0` keeps the page scrolling through most of the sample instead of counting ambient-rendering frames during the default idle delays.
The chat scenario is the phone's longest Agent session (largest transcript); the first round used the Persona main chat, which rendered only about 20 frames each time (it never started scrolling), so it does not count.

With Haze's default blur input resolution (about 0.78):

| Scenario | Style | Sample frames | min | p50 | p90 | max | mean (ms) | Jank rate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Agents list | Brief | 874 | 2.6 | 8.5 | 16.7 | 33.6 | 9.45 | 4.46% (3.4 / 4.5 / 6.1) |
| Agents list | Board | 993 | 2.8 | 7.1 | 10.3 | 32.2 | 8.03 | 1.02% (0.2 / 2.3 / 4.6) |
| Agents list | Glass | 988 | 5.2 | 10.9 | 15.5 | 51.1 | 11.31 | 1.75% (0.3 / 2.3 / 4.4) |
| Inbox | Brief | 763 | 2.6 | 8.6 | 17.7 | 54.0 | 10.46 | 7.08% (4.1 / 6.8 / 9.2) |
| Inbox | Board | 992 | 2.6 | 7.0 | 10.9 | 43.5 | 8.34 | 1.38% (0.0 / 3.1 / 5.3) |
| Inbox | Glass | 772 | 4.6 | 11.2 | 18.8 | 54.2 | 12.99 | 6.59% (5.1 / 6.4 / 7.9) |
| Chat | Brief | 1183 | 2.8 | 8.2 | 12.5 | 83.3 | 9.27 | 5.53% (3.6 / 5.5 / 8.4) |
| Chat | Board | 1182 | 2.4 | 8.1 | 13.7 | 90.5 | 9.39 | 5.32% (3.9 / 5.1 / 9.2) |
| Chat | Glass | 1186 | 7.3 | 16.3 | 22.3 | 60.2 | 17.46 | 4.39% (2.0 / 4.9 / 8.8) |

Conclusion: on list pages glass adds 2–4 ms to median frame time over the other two styles, with a jank rate comparable to Brief. The chat page has the most overlays (top bar, task strip, status line, composer and two scroll edges),
and glass's median frame time is twice the other styles'. Blur input was therefore reduced to quarter resolution (`LocalHazePerformanceMode` = `Performance`, `PersonaTheme`),
with no visible difference in screenshot comparisons. Phone results after lowering resolution: not measured (the phone dropped adb halfway).

Emulator figures are only a reference (emulated GPU, very slow RenderEffect): glass median frame time is 166 ms for Agents and 261 ms for chat, versus about 17 ms for Brief and Board;
at quarter resolution glass is 103 / 126 ms. The fallback below normally takes over on such a machine.

Fallback: severe sustained jank (over 60% slow frames after at least 120 frames) drops one level early; otherwise the original window applies (over 30% of 240 frames exceed 1.5 times the frame budget). Each drop is one level for this run (refraction → blur → opaque);
battery saver and "Reduce transparency" use opaque panels directly (verified on the emulator by unplugging and enabling battery saver: floating dock and top bar become opaque, scroll edges become gradients). Battery usage: not measured.

## Verification records

These are historical records for the old App; they were not repeated during this documentation check.

Emulator (Android 14), connected to a real hub, with a Linux machine running the hub and a Mac connected over SSH:

- Pairing from a scanned link and exchanging for a device token; both machines online, real sessions in the list
- Sending from the phone to claude in herdr on the Mac; claude's AskUserQuestion becomes a card (`question` when intercepted by the hook, `screen` in the TUI), and claude receives the selection made on the phone
- Approving the ExitPlanMode dialog on the phone in plan mode; claude continues and edits files
- App in the background: choosing an option directly on a question notification reaches claude; finishing a turn raises "Finished"
- Automatic reconnect within seconds after a hub restart; paging back two days in a long session; dark-mode screenshot inspection

Real device (Redmi K60 Pro, Android 16, HyperOS 3, no Google services), phone connected to the hub over USB (`adb reverse tcp:18660 tcp:18660`):

- Pairing, list and chat page work; answering claude's question on the phone reaches claude on the computer
- "Needs you" notifications exist in the system (channel `input`, highest importance), but do not appear in the notification area by default: HyperOS disables floating / lock-screen notifications for newly installed Apps,
  so they must be enabled in Settings › Notifications and Control center › Notification management › Persona. Long-running background connectivity after locking the screen has not yet been tested on a device

## Avatar (Persona's face)

The only built-in drawing is **Mochi** (`persona`), recreated from the App icon with Canvas in `core/designsystem/PersonaFace`. Its geometry and light / dark colors match the other clients and stay sharp at any size. The other bundled option is **Milk Frog** (`nwverse`), downloaded from the hub's built-in pack. Visor, Circuit, Pixel, Facet and Jelly are retired; saved choices fall back to Mochi.

- Used in: the top-left of the Persona page top bar (`JPersonaButton`, replacing the old **Chats** icon; tapping still opens the chat list; accessibility reads **Chats** and "Persona, <state>"), and the top of the chat list (formerly a letter avatar, also used in the persistent sidebar on wide screens). Both show the same state.
- Five states: idle (blinks every 3.6 seconds), thinking (eyes look up-right, three thought-bubble dots light in turn), working (eyes scan left and right), needs you (breathing ring), offline (gray, eyes closed to lines). Only the last three animate continuously (about 12 frames/second); idle moves only when blinking. With "Reduce motion" or battery saver each state stays on a still frame (like iOS low power mode).
- State derivation (`personaState` in `feature/assistant`, same rules as iOS `AppModel.personaState`): unable to reach the hub (connecting, waiting for network, etc.; "Updating" doesn't count) or this chat's machine offline means offline; a chat waiting for you (`needs_you` or a pending interaction) means needs you; a running turn means working; otherwise idle. It looks at the currently open chat; a new-chat page has no chat, so it only checks whether Persona's machine is online and is otherwise idle.
